Yeah… I mean, I’m still keeping up overall. It’s not like everything’s falling apart, you know? Work is getting done, bills are paid, that kind of thing.

Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably.

Like with my wife, if she’s on the phone with her parents or talking to them when we’re together, I’ll jump in before she’s done explaining something. I’ll think I’m helping clarify, but then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like I stepped on her conversation.

And at work too, I’ll finish an employee’s thought before they’re actually finished saying it. In my head I’m like, “I know where this is going,” but sometimes I don’t, and even when I do, it rubs people the wrong way. I don’t usually mean anything by it… it just comes out before I’ve really thought about how it’s going to land.

I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question, which… I guess I thought I was being helpful, but it probably drove them crazy. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult. I just had a hard time holding it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things you're thinking without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so, but maybe in a different way.

The focus stuff bothers me because I know when I’m not getting something done the way I should. But the restlessness and the blurting things out… that’s more visible to other people, you know? Like I’m still keeping up overall, work is still functioning, home is still functioning, but it’s starting to cost me a little.

At home, it’s trust, I guess. If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”

If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.

Like, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.

So I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.

Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.

When I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.

If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. Teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt things out before they were even done asking the question. I remember thinking it was kind of funny at the time, like, “Why does it matter where I sit?” But yeah… it was there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in a big way, no. I mean, I guess I’ve gotten better at covering it or working around it, but it’s not like it disappeared as I got older.

Funny thing is, I remember being more annoyed about where they moved me than about why they were moving me… but yeah, that pattern was there pretty early.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… over the past year or so, I’m getting pulled off track by the smallest things. Like my phone buzzes, or someone walks past my office, or even just some random thought pops in—like something about my in-laws, which is a whole other story—and suddenly I’m not doing the thing I was supposed to be doing.  \n\nIt’s been happening enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks even when there are no distractions around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even if it’s quiet, like if I sit down to work on a planning document, I’ll be good for the first few minutes and then somehow I’m just… not really with it anymore.\n\nSame thing in meetings with my managers. I’ll start out locked in, and then a few minutes later I realize I missed half of what they said, and I have to ask them to go back over it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the one steering the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not every second, but especially when I’m supposed to just sit still, like at family dinners or these church volunteer meetings I go to. I’ll be sitting there and it feels like there’s a motor running inside me or something.\n\nI end up tapping my foot, messing with a pen, folding a napkin, anything just to keep my hands busy. And then I’ll catch myself thinking about, like, the parking situation at church or who’s bringing what to the next event, and I realize I’m barely sitting still. It’s uncomfortable, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and restlessness causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still keeping up overall. It’s not like everything’s falling apart, you know? Work is getting done, bills are paid, that kind of thing.\n\nBut it is starting to cause problems. At work, people are getting less patient with me in meetings when I lose the thread or when a mentoring conversation kind of goes sideways because I didn’t stay on the point. And at home, it’s more like… trust, I guess. If we make family plans and I don’t follow through the way I said I would, or I miss part of what was decided, it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then I end up trying to explain myself, but I ramble, and honestly that probably makes it worse. So yeah, I’m managing, but it’s costing me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have problems with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think so. I mean, at the time I didn’t think of it that way—I was just “that kid,” you know?\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question, which… I guess I thought I was being helpful, but it probably drove them crazy. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult. I just had a hard time holding it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things you're thinking without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s on the phone with her parents or talking to them when we’re together, I’ll jump in before she’s done explaining something. I’ll think I’m helping clarify, but then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like I stepped on her conversation.\n\nAnd at work too, I’ll finish an employee’s thought before they’re actually finished saying it. In my head I’m like, “I know where this is going,” but sometimes I don’t, and even when I do, it rubs people the wrong way. I don’t usually mean anything by it… it just comes out before I’ve really thought about how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity pretty consistently since you were a child, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… some of it’s been there since I was a kid, like I said, but it’s been noticeably harder to manage recently.\n\nI’d say for about… a year and a couple months? Maybe a year and two months. That’s around when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life, and since then it’s like I don’t have as much room to keep it together. Like before, I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I was. But with all that going on, it’s been harder to stay steady.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as bothersome to you and those around you as your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but maybe in a different way.\n\nThe focus stuff bothers me because I know when I’m not getting something done the way I should. But the restlessness and the blurting things out… that’s more visible to other people, you know? Like I’m still keeping up overall, work is still functioning, home is still functioning, but it’s starting to cost me a little.\n\nAt home, it’s trust, I guess. If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. And at home, it’s more like… trust, I guess. If we make family plans and I don’t follow through the way I said I would, or I miss part of what was decided, it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then I end up trying to explain myself, but I ramble, and honestly that probably makes it worse. So yeah, I’m managing, but it’s costing me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have problems with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think so. I mean, at the time I didn’t think of it that way—I was just “that kid,” you know?\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question, which… I guess I thought I was being helpful, but it probably drove them crazy. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult. I just had a hard time holding it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things you're thinking without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s on the phone with her parents or talking to them when we’re together, I’ll jump in before she’s done explaining something. I’ll think I’m helping clarify, but then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like I stepped on her conversation.\n\nAnd at work too, I’ll finish an employee’s thought before they’re actually finished saying it. In my head I’m like, “I know where this is going,” but sometimes I don’t, and even when I do, it rubs people the wrong way. I don’t usually mean anything by it… it just comes out before I’ve really thought about how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity pretty consistently since you were a child, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… some of it’s been there since I was a kid, like I said, but it’s been noticeably harder to manage recently.\n\nI’d say for about… a year and a couple months? Maybe a year and two months. That’s around when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life, and since then it’s like I don’t have as much room to keep it together. Like before, I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I was. But with all that going on, it’s been harder to stay steady.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as bothersome to you and those around you as your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but maybe in a different way.\n\nThe focus stuff bothers me because I know when I’m not getting something done the way I should. But the restlessness and the blurting things out… that’s more visible to other people, you know? Like I’m still keeping up overall, work is still functioning, home is still functioning, but it’s starting to cost me a little.\n\nAt home, it’s trust, I guess. If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. But yeah, the restlessness and blurting things out were definitely there when I was young too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, like a year or more, where you didn't struggle with these focus, restlessness, and impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was ever like a whole year where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nI mean, looking back, it goes way back to when I was a kid. Teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt things out before they were even done asking the question. I remember thinking it was kind of funny at the time, like, “Why does it matter where I sit?” But yeah… it was there. I don’t really remember a long stretch where it just went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in these symptoms as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big way, no. I mean, I guess I’ve gotten better at covering it or working around it, but it’s not like it disappeared as I got older.\n\nLooking back, it was around when I was a kid too—teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt something out before they even finished the question. Funny thing is, I remember being more annoyed about where they moved me than about why they were moving me… but yeah, that pattern was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a friendship or a significant strain in a relationship?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s ended a friendship exactly, but it’s definitely strained things.\n\nAt home, it’s more like… trust takes a hit. Like family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to, and then it becomes this whole thing where people feel like they can’t count on me the same way. And at work, I’m still keeping up overall—I mean, things are getting done—but in meetings or mentoring conversations, if they go sideways, I can tell people’s patience wears thin.\n\nIt’s not like everything’s falling apart, but it’s starting to cost me with people, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

If we make family plans and I don’t follow through the way I said I would, or I miss part of what was decided, it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then I end up trying to explain myself, but I ramble, and honestly that probably makes it worse. So yeah, I’m managing, but it’s costing me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have problems with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think so. I mean, at the time I didn’t think of it that way—I was just “that kid,” you know?\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question, which… I guess I thought I was being helpful, but it probably drove them crazy. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult. I just had a hard time holding it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things you're thinking without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s on the phone with her parents or talking to them when we’re together, I’ll jump in before she’s done explaining something. I’ll think I’m helping clarify, but then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like I stepped on her conversation.\n\nAnd at work too, I’ll finish an employee’s thought before they’re actually finished saying it. In my head I’m like, “I know where this is going,” but sometimes I don’t, and even when I do, it rubs people the wrong way. I don’t usually mean anything by it… it just comes out before I’ve really thought about how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity pretty consistently since you were a child, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… some of it’s been there since I was a kid, like I said, but it’s been noticeably harder to manage recently.\n\nI’d say for about… a year and a couple months? Maybe a year and two months. That’s around when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life, and since then it’s like I don’t have as much room to keep it together. Like before, I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I was. But with all that going on, it’s been harder to stay steady.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as bothersome to you and those around you as your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but maybe in a different way.\n\nThe focus stuff bothers me because I know when I’m not getting something done the way I should. But the restlessness and the blurting things out… that’s more visible to other people, you know? Like I’m still keeping up overall, work is still functioning, home is still functioning, but it’s starting to cost me a little.\n\nAt home, it’s trust, I guess. If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. But yeah, the restlessness and blurting things out were definitely there when I was young too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, like a year or more, where you didn't struggle with these focus, restlessness, and impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was ever like a whole year where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nI mean, looking back, it goes way back to when I was a kid. Teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt things out before they were even done asking the question. I remember thinking it was kind of funny at the time, like, “Why does it matter where I sit?” But yeah… it was there. I don’t really remember a long stretch where it just went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in these symptoms as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big way, no. I mean, I guess I’ve gotten better at covering it or working around it, but it’s not like it disappeared as I got older.\n\nLooking back, it was around when I was a kid too—teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt something out before they even finished the question. Funny thing is, I remember being more annoyed about where they moved me than about why they were moving me… but yeah, that pattern was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a friendship or a significant strain in a relationship?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s ended a friendship exactly, but it’s definitely strained things.\n\nAt home, it’s more like… trust takes a hit. Like family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to, and then it becomes this whole thing where people feel like they can’t count on me the same way. And at work, I’m still keeping up overall—I mean, things are getting done—but in meetings or mentoring conversations, if they go sideways, I can tell people’s patience wears thin.\n\nIt’s not like everything’s falling apart, but it’s starting to cost me with people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or at home, to the point where you felt like you couldn't function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to function, no. I’m still keeping up overall… like things get done, most of the time.\n\nBut it’s starting to have a cost. At home, if family plans don’t go the way they should because I’m scattered or I change gears too fast, people get frustrated and it chips away at trust. And at work, especially in meetings or mentoring conversations, if things get off track, I can feel people’s patience wearing thin.\n\nSo not like I can’t function at all, but it’s definitely affecting how smoothly things run.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely longer than six months. It’s been… I’d say noticeably harder to manage for about a year and a couple months now.\n\nIt kind of picked up when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life. And I know that sounds like a whole separate drama—because it is, honestly—but that’s around when I really started feeling like I couldn’t keep things as contained as before. So yeah, about a year and two months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

And at work, especially in meetings or mentoring conversations, if things get off track, I can feel people’s patience wearing thin.\n\nSo not like I can’t function at all, but it’s definitely affecting how smoothly things run.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely longer than six months. It’s been… I’d say noticeably harder to manage for about a year and a couple months now.\n\nIt kind of picked up when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life. And I know that sounds like a whole separate drama—because it is, honestly—but that’s around when I really started feeling like I couldn’t keep things as contained as before. So yeah, about a year and two months.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of at least a few years where you didn't experience any of these focus, restlessness, or impulsivity issues?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t really think of my life in chunks like, “those three years were fine,” you know?

I mean, there have been stretches where things felt more manageable, or where nobody was really pointing it out as a problem. But whether that means I didn’t have the issues at all for a few years… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it more, because it kind of depends what counts as “didn’t experience it.”

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… over the past year or so, I’m getting pulled off track by the smallest things. Even if it’s quiet, like if I sit down to work on a planning document, I’ll be good for the first few minutes and then somehow I’m just… not really with it anymore.\n\nSame thing in meetings with my managers. I’ll start out locked in, and then a few minutes later I realize I missed half of what they said, and I have to ask them to go back over it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the one steering the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not every second, but especially when I’m supposed to just sit still, like at family dinners or these church volunteer meetings I go to. I’ll be sitting there and it feels like there’s a motor running inside me or something.\n\nI end up tapping my foot, messing with a pen, folding a napkin, anything just to keep my hands busy. And then I’ll catch myself thinking about, like, the parking situation at church or who’s bringing what to the next event, and I realize I’m barely sitting still. It’s uncomfortable, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and restlessness causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still keeping up overall. It’s not like everything’s falling apart, you know? Work is getting done, bills are paid, that kind of thing.\n\nBut it is starting to cause problems. If we make family plans and I don’t follow through the way I said I would, or I miss part of what was decided, it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then I end up trying to explain myself, but I ramble, and honestly that probably makes it worse. So yeah, I’m managing, but it’s costing me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have problems with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think so. I mean, at the time I didn’t think of it that way—I was just “that kid,” you know?\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question, which… I guess I thought I was being helpful, but it probably drove them crazy. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult. I just had a hard time holding it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things you're thinking without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s on the phone with her parents or talking to them when we’re together, I’ll jump in before she’s done explaining something. I’ll think I’m helping clarify, but then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like I stepped on her conversation.\n\nAnd at work too, I’ll finish an employee’s thought before they’re actually finished saying it. In my head I’m like, “I know where this is going,” but sometimes I don’t, and even when I do, it rubs people the wrong way. I don’t usually mean anything by it… it just comes out before I’ve really thought about how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity pretty consistently since you were a child, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… some of it’s been there since I was a kid, like I said, but it’s been noticeably harder to manage recently.\n\nI’d say for about… a year and a couple months? Maybe a year and two months. That’s around when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life, and since then it’s like I don’t have as much room to keep it together. Like before, I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I was. But with all that going on, it’s been harder to stay steady.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as bothersome to you and those around you as your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but maybe in a different way.\n\nThe focus stuff bothers me because I know when I’m not getting something done the way I should. But the restlessness and the blurting things out… that’s more visible to other people, you know? Like I’m still keeping up overall, work is still functioning, home is still functioning, but it’s starting to cost me a little.\n\nAt home, it’s trust, I guess. If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. But yeah, the restlessness and blurting things out were definitely there when I was young too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, like a year or more, where you didn't struggle with these focus, restlessness, and impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was ever like a whole year where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nI mean, looking back, it goes way back to when I was a kid. Teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt things out before they were even done asking the question. I remember thinking it was kind of funny at the time, like, “Why does it matter where I sit?” But yeah… it was there. I don’t really remember a long stretch where it just went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in these symptoms as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big way, no. I’m not sure, honestly. Like, if you mean a time where it was completely gone, zero issues… I don’t really know that I can point to that.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… over the past year or so, I’m getting pulled off track by the smallest things. Like my phone buzzes, or someone walks past my office, or even just some random thought pops in—like something about my in-laws, which is a whole other story—and suddenly I’m not doing the thing I was supposed to be doing.  \n\nIt’s been happening enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks even when there are no distractions around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even if it’s quiet, like if I sit down to work on a planning document, I’ll be good for the first few minutes and then somehow I’m just… not really with it anymore.\n\nSame thing in meetings with my managers. I’ll start out locked in, and then a few minutes later I realize I missed half of what they said, and I have to ask them to go back over it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the one steering the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not every second, but especially when I’m supposed to just sit still, like at family dinners or these church volunteer meetings I go to. I’ll be sitting there and it feels like there’s a motor running inside me or something.\n\nI end up tapping my foot, messing with a pen, folding a napkin, anything just to keep my hands busy. And then I’ll catch myself thinking about, like, the parking situation at church or who’s bringing what to the next event, and I realize I’m barely sitting still. It’s uncomfortable, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and restlessness causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still keeping up overall. It’s not like everything’s falling apart, you know? Work is getting done, bills are paid, that kind of thing.\n\nBut it is starting to cause problems. At work, people are getting less patient with me in meetings when I lose the thread or when a mentoring conversation kind of goes sideways because I didn’t stay on the point. And at home, it’s more like… trust, I guess. If we make family plans and I don’t follow through the way I said I would, or I miss part of what was decided, it turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then I end up trying to explain myself, but I ramble, and honestly that probably makes it worse. So yeah, I’m managing, but it’s costing me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have problems with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think so. I mean, at the time I didn’t think of it that way—I was just “that kid,” you know?\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question, which… I guess I thought I was being helpful, but it probably drove them crazy. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult. I just had a hard time holding it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things you're thinking without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s on the phone with her parents or talking to them when we’re together, I’ll jump in before she’s done explaining something. I’ll think I’m helping clarify, but then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like I stepped on her conversation.\n\nAnd at work too, I’ll finish an employee’s thought before they’re actually finished saying it. In my head I’m like, “I know where this is going,” but sometimes I don’t, and even when I do, it rubs people the wrong way. I don’t usually mean anything by it… it just comes out before I’ve really thought about how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity pretty consistently since you were a child, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… some of it’s been there since I was a kid, like I said, but it’s been noticeably harder to manage recently.\n\nI’d say for about… a year and a couple months? Maybe a year and two months. That’s around when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life, and since then it’s like I don’t have as much room to keep it together. Like before, I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I was. But with all that going on, it’s been harder to stay steady.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as bothersome to you and those around you as your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but maybe in a different way.\n\nThe focus stuff bothers me because I know when I’m not getting something done the way I should. But the restlessness and the blurting things out… that’s more visible to other people, you know? Like I’m still keeping up overall, work is still functioning, home is still functioning, but it’s starting to cost me a little.\n\nAt home, it’s trust, I guess. If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. But yeah, the restlessness and blurting things out were definitely there when I was young too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, like a year or more, where you didn't struggle with these focus, restlessness, and impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was ever like a whole year where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nI mean, looking back, it goes way back to when I was a kid. Teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt things out before they were even done asking the question. I remember thinking it was kind of funny at the time, like, “Why does it matter where I sit?” But yeah… it was there. I don’t really remember a long stretch where it just went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in these symptoms as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big way, no. I mean, I guess I’ve gotten better at covering it or working around it, but it’s not like it disappeared as I got older.\n\nLooking back, it was around when I was a kid too—teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt something out before they even finished the question. Funny thing is, I remember being more annoyed about where they moved me than about why they were moving me… but yeah, that pattern was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a friendship or a significant strain in a relationship?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s ended a friendship exactly, but it’s definitely strained things.\n\nAt home, it’s more like… trust takes a hit. Like family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to, and then it becomes this whole thing where people feel like they can’t count on me the same way. And at work, I’m still keeping up overall—I mean, things are getting done—but in meetings or mentoring conversations, if they go sideways, I can tell people’s patience wears thin.\n\nIt’s not like everything’s falling apart, but it’s starting to cost me with people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or at home, to the point where you felt like you couldn't function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to function, no. I’m still keeping up overall… like things get done, most of the time.\n\nBut it’s starting to have a cost. At home, if family plans don’t go the way they should because I’m scattered or I change gears too fast, people get frustrated and it chips away at trust. In my head I’m like, “I know where this is going,” but sometimes I don’t, and even when I do, it rubs people the wrong way. I don’t usually mean anything by it… it just comes out before I’ve really thought about how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity pretty consistently since you were a child, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… some of it’s been there since I was a kid, like I said, but it’s been noticeably harder to manage recently.\n\nI’d say for about… a year and a couple months? Maybe a year and two months. That’s around when the conflict with my in-laws started taking over more of our family life, and since then it’s like I don’t have as much room to keep it together. Like before, I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I was. But with all that going on, it’s been harder to stay steady.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as bothersome to you and those around you as your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but maybe in a different way.\n\nThe focus stuff bothers me because I know when I’m not getting something done the way I should. But the restlessness and the blurting things out… that’s more visible to other people, you know? Like I’m still keeping up overall, work is still functioning, home is still functioning, but it’s starting to cost me a little.\n\nAt home, it’s trust, I guess. If family plans don’t go the way they should because I jump in too fast or push something along without thinking it through, then it becomes this whole thing. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people lose patience with me. Not like everything’s falling apart, but enough that I notice it afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are causing more problems in your daily life than your difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, in some ways they are causing more problems, even if I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nLike, the focus stuff is frustrating, but the impulsive parts are what people react to more. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. But yeah, the restlessness and blurting things out were definitely there when I was young too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, like a year or more, where you didn't struggle with these focus, restlessness, and impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was ever like a whole year where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nI mean, looking back, it goes way back to when I was a kid. Teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt things out before they were even done asking the question. I remember thinking it was kind of funny at the time, like, “Why does it matter where I sit?” But yeah… it was there. I don’t really remember a long stretch where it just went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in these symptoms as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big way, no. I mean, I guess I’ve gotten better at covering it or working around it, but it’s not like it disappeared as I got older.\n\nLooking back, it was around when I was a kid too—teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt something out before they even finished the question. Funny thing is, I remember being more annoyed about where they moved me than about why they were moving me… but yeah, that pattern was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a friendship or a significant strain in a relationship?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s ended a friendship exactly, but it’s definitely strained things.\n\nAt home, it’s more like… trust takes a hit. Like family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to, and then it becomes this whole thing where people feel like they can’t count on me the same way. And at work, I’m still keeping up overall—I mean, things are getting done—but in meetings or mentoring conversations, if they go sideways, I can tell people’s patience wears thin.\n\nIt’s not like everything’s falling apart, but it’s starting to cost me with people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or at home, to the point where you felt like you couldn't function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to function, no. And I know that sounds like a whole separate drama—because it is, honestly—but that’s around when I really started feeling like I couldn’t keep things as contained as before. So yeah, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few years where you didn't experience any of these focus, restlessness, or impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. If I jump ahead in a meeting or cut someone off when I’m trying to mentor them, I can feel the patience kind of leave the room. And at home, if I push a family plan along too fast or say something before I’ve really thought it through, it affects trust. Then I’m trying to explain what I meant, and it turns into a bigger thing than it needed to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my life is falling apart or anything, but yeah… it’s starting to cost me more than just being distracted does.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it wasn’t like this just appeared out of nowhere.\n\nWhen I was a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they even finished the question. I remember that pretty clearly, actually. They’d put me closer to the front, then away from certain kids, then by myself sometimes… which, honestly, I usually still found a way to talk.\n\nI don’t know if there were “significant improvements” exactly. I think I just learned ways to get by as I got older, or people tolerated it differently. But yeah, the restlessness and blurting things out were definitely there when I was young too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, like a year or more, where you didn't struggle with these focus, restlessness, and impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was ever like a whole year where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nI mean, looking back, it goes way back to when I was a kid. Teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt things out before they were even done asking the question. I remember thinking it was kind of funny at the time, like, “Why does it matter where I sit?” But yeah… it was there. I don’t really remember a long stretch where it just went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in these symptoms as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big way, no. I mean, I guess I’ve gotten better at covering it or working around it, but it’s not like it disappeared as I got older.\n\nLooking back, it was around when I was a kid too—teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt something out before they even finished the question. Funny thing is, I remember being more annoyed about where they moved me than about why they were moving me… but yeah, that pattern was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to the end of a friendship or a significant strain in a relationship?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s ended a friendship exactly, but it’s definitely strained things.\n\nAt home, it’s more like… trust takes a hit. Like family plans don’t go the way they’re supposed to, and then it becomes this whole thing where people feel like they can’t count on me the same way. And at work, I’m still keeping up overall—I mean, things are getting done—but in meetings or mentoring conversations, if they go sideways, I can tell people’s patience wears thin.\n\nIt’s not like everything’s falling apart, but it’s starting to cost me with people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or at home, to the point where you felt like you couldn't function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to function, no. I’m still keeping up overall… like things get done, most of the time.\n\nBut it’s starting to have a cost. At home, if family plans don’t go the way they should because I’m scattered or I change gears too fast, people get frustrated and it chips away at trust. And at work, especially in meetings or mentoring conversations, if things get off track, I can feel people’s patience wearing thin.\n\nSo not like I can’t function at all, but it’s definitely affecting how smoothly things run.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely longer than six months. I don’t really think of my life in chunks like, “those three years were fine,” you know?\n\nI mean, there have been stretches where things felt more manageable, or where nobody was really pointing it out as a problem. But whether that means I didn’t have the issues at all for a few years… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it more, because it kind of depends what counts as “didn’t experience it.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt like you didn't have any of these focus, restlessness, or impulsivity issues at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few years where you didn't experience any of these focus, restlessness, or impulsivity issues?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t really think of my life in chunks like, “those three years were fine,” you know?\n\nI mean, there have been stretches where things felt more manageable, or where nobody was really pointing it out as a problem. But whether that means I didn’t have the issues at all for a few years… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it more, because it kind of depends what counts as “didn’t experience it.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt like you didn't have any of these focus, restlessness, or impulsivity issues at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, if you mean a time where it was completely gone, zero issues… I don’t really know that I can point to that.\n\nThere were times where life was calmer, or I was busier in a way that sort of kept me moving, if that makes sense. I remember at one job—well, not the job itself, but that period—everything had a rhythm, and I probably would’ve said I was fine. But then again, I don’t know if that means the issues weren’t there, or just nobody was asking me about them.\n\nSo… I don’t have a clear “yes, there was a period where none of this existed.” I’d have to think harder about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity in all areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it shows up in more than one place. I mean, I’m still functioning, I’m still getting things done overall — it’s not like everything is falling apart.\n\nBut at home, it’s starting to cost me trust, if that makes sense. Like family plans don’t always go the way they should, and then people start expecting me to mess something up or get sidetracked. And at work… I run things, so there’s a lot of meetings and mentoring conversations, and I can usually cover it, but people’s patience wears thin when I’m not staying with the conversation the way I should.\n\nSocially, I guess it’s less obvious, or maybe people just laugh it off more. But yeah, it’s not just one setting. It kind of leaks into everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced significant difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity consistently over the past year?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been noticeably harder to manage for about a year and two months, since the in-law conflict started taking over more of our family life.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… over the past year, yes. I’d say maybe a little more than a year, actually — like a year and two months or so.

It got noticeably harder to manage once the in-law conflict started taking up more space in our family life. And I don’t mean every single minute of every day, but since that whole situation became more of a constant thing, it’s been harder to keep things under control. Before that, I might’ve said it was there but manageable. Lately it’s been harder to ignore.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
